People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I know that sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: It’s not all the time, exactly… mostly when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it hits me the most, like I’m seeing everything through glass or I’m not quite in my body. The buildings and students feel kind of unreal for a bit. I know that probably sounds odd, but it’s been happening enough that it’s scaring me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations when you feel like you're watching everything through glass?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure if it’s physical exactly. It’s more like… when I’m walking across campus, I suddenly feel like there’s this layer between me and everything else, like I’m watching through glass. I don’t feel fully in my own body, and the buildings and people around me seem kind of unreal for a moment. That’s the part that scares me most, honestly… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're walking across campus and everything feels unreal?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think anxious is fair. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s the part that scares me most—when I’m walking across campus and it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body. The buildings and students around me just feel slightly unreal, and then I get worried that something is really wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body or things seem unreal when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least, not that I’ve noticed in the same way. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students seem a little unreal. I’m sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part that scares me most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience emotions as strongly as you used to?\n\nPatient: I… I think maybe not as strongly, no. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know people are trying to be kind. Like, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was really sweet. When I’m walking across campus, it can feel like I’m sort of watching everything through glass—like I’m not fully in my own body. The buildings and the students around me feel… slightly unreal, if that makes sense. I know that sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: It’s not all the time, exactly… mostly when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it hits me the most, like I’m seeing everything through glass or I’m not quite in my body. The buildings and students feel kind of unreal for a bit. I know that probably sounds odd, but it’s been happening enough that it’s scaring me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations when you feel like you're watching everything through glass?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure if it’s physical exactly. It’s more like… when I’m walking across campus, I suddenly feel like there’s this layer between me and everything else, like I’m watching through glass. I don’t feel fully in my own body, and the buildings and people around me seem kind of unreal for a moment. That’s the part that scares me most, honestly… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're walking across campus and everything feels unreal?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think anxious is fair. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s the part that scares me most—when I’m walking across campus and it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body. The buildings and students around me just feel slightly unreal, and then I get worried that something is really wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body or things seem unreal when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least, not that I’ve noticed in the same way. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students seem a little unreal. I’m sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part that scares me most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience emotions as strongly as you used to?\n\nPatient: I… I think maybe not as strongly, no. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know people are trying to be kind. Like, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was really sweet. I could recognize it, if that makes sense. But I couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure I normally would. It was more like this blank heaviness. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel numb or disconnected from your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not all the time, but enough that I notice it. Like I said, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was kind—I could tell myself, “This is sweet, I should feel touched.” But I just… couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure of it. It was more like this blank heaviness. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really happens much outside of that, no… or not in a way I can clearly point to. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus that it gets frightening—like I’m seeing everything through glass, and I’m not quite inside myself. The buildings and the students around me feel just a little unreal. I know that probably sounds odd, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, nothing I would want to label that way, I don’t think. I’ve had stressful things happen, like anyone, but I don’t know if they count as traumatic.\n\nIs it okay if I say I’m not sure?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I said, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was kind—I could tell myself, “This is sweet, I should feel touched.” But I just… couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure of it. It was more like this blank heaviness. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really happens much outside of that, no… or not in a way I can clearly point to. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus that it gets frightening—like I’m seeing everything through glass, and I’m not quite inside myself. The buildings and the students around me feel just a little unreal. I know that probably sounds odd, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, nothing I would want to label that way, I don’t think. I’ve had stressful things happen, like anyone, but I don’t know if they count as traumatic.\n\nIs it okay if I say I’m not sure?\n\nDoctor: Are there things from your past that you try to avoid thinking or talking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. The buildings and the students around me feel… slightly unreal, if that makes sense. It’s more like… when I’m walking across campus, I suddenly feel like there’s this layer between me and everything else, like I’m watching through glass. I don’t feel fully in my own body, and the buildings and people around me seem kind of unreal for a moment. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s the part that scares me most—when I’m walking across campus and it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students seem a little unreal. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know people are trying to be kind. Like, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was really sweet. It was more like this blank heaviness. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really happens much outside of that, no… or not in a way I can clearly point to. The buildings and students around me just feel slightly unreal, and then I get worried that something is really wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body or things seem unreal when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least, not that I’ve noticed in the same way. I’m sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part that scares me most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience emotions as strongly as you used to?\n\nPatient: I… I think maybe not as strongly, no. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know people are trying to be kind. Like, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was really sweet. I could recognize it, if that makes sense. But I couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure I normally would. It was more like this blank heaviness. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel numb or disconnected from your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not all the time, but enough that I notice it. Like I said, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was kind—I could tell myself, “This is sweet, I should feel touched.” But I just… couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure of it. It was more like this blank heaviness. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really happens much outside of that, no… or not in a way I can clearly point to. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus that it gets frightening—like I’m seeing everything through glass, and I’m not quite inside myself. The buildings and the students around me feel just a little unreal. I know that probably sounds odd, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, nothing I would want to label that way, I don’t think. I’ve had stressful things happen, like anyone, but I don’t know if they count as traumatic.\n\nIs it okay if I say I’m not sure?\n\nDoctor: Are there things from your past that you try to avoid thinking or talking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, there are things I don’t love dwelling on, but I wouldn’t say there’s something specific from my past that I’m actively avoiding talking about.\n\nI hope that’s an okay answer—I’m trying to be honest, I just don’t know that there’s anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're doing everyday activities at home?\n\nPatient: At home? Not really… or at least not in the same way. I might feel a little spaced out sometimes, but the thing that really scares me is when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem slightly unreal.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange. I’m sorry—I’m trying to explain it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings is triggered by something specific about walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I think it might be something about being out there in the middle of campus, yes. I don’t know exactly what triggers it, but when I’m walking between buildings and there are students around, that’s when it can suddenly feel like I’m behind glass or not quite in my body.\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it that way, and I don’t want to overstate it, but it really does scare me when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same way if you were walking alone in a quiet area?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, but I think it would probably be less intense if I were alone somewhere quiet. The part that really seems to scare me is crossing campus with the buildings and people around me — that’s when it starts feeling like I’m watching everything through glass, or like I’m not fully in my body.\n\nI know that sounds odd… I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings is related to feeling anxious or overwhelmed in that specific situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is related to feeling overwhelmed, at least in that situation. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but when I’m walking across campus, that’s what scares me most — it suddenly feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem a little unreal.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s the “right” way to describe it, but it does seem to happen when I’m out there and feeling kind of flooded by everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about walking across campus that you find particularly stressful or worrying?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but it’s hard to pin down exactly. It’s not one single thing like a particular building or spot, I don’t think. It’s more that when I’m walking across campus with everything around me — the buildings, the students passing by — that’s when I get really scared because it starts to feel like I’m watching it all through glass.\n\nAnd I feel a little embarrassed saying it, but it’s like I’m not fully in my own body, and everything around me seems just slightly unreal. I’m not sure if that answers the question properly, but that’s the part that worries me most.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I said, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was kind—I could tell myself, “This is sweet, I should feel touched.” But I just… couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure of it. It was more like this blank heaviness. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really happens much outside of that, no… or not in a way I can clearly point to. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus that it gets frightening—like I’m seeing everything through glass, and I’m not quite inside myself. The buildings and the students around me feel just a little unreal. I know that probably sounds odd, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, nothing I would want to label that way, I don’t think. I’ve had stressful things happen, like anyone, but I don’t know if they count as traumatic.\n\nIs it okay if I say I’m not sure?\n\nDoctor: Are there things from your past that you try to avoid thinking or talking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, there are things I don’t love dwelling on, but I wouldn’t say there’s something specific from my past that I’m actively avoiding talking about.\n\nI hope that’s an okay answer—I’m trying to be honest, I just don’t know that there’s anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're doing everyday activities at home?\n\nPatient: At home? Not really… or at least not in the same way. I might feel a little spaced out sometimes, but the thing that really scares me is when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem slightly unreal.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange. I’m sorry—I’m trying to explain it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings is triggered by something specific about walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I think it might be something about being out there in the middle of campus, yes. I don’t know exactly what triggers it, but when I’m walking between buildings and there are students around, that’s when it can suddenly feel like I’m behind glass or not quite in my body.\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it that way, and I don’t want to overstate it, but it really does scare me when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same way if you were walking alone in a quiet area?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, but I think it would probably be less intense if I were alone somewhere quiet. The part that really seems to scare me is crossing campus with the buildings and people around me — that’s when it starts feeling like I’m watching everything through glass, or like I’m not fully in my body.\n\nI know that sounds odd… I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings is related to feeling anxious or overwhelmed in that specific situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is related to feeling overwhelmed, at least in that situation. I could recognize it, if that makes sense. But I couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure I normally would. It was more like this blank heaviness. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel numb or disconnected from your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not all the time, but enough that I notice it. Like I said, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was kind—I could tell myself, “This is sweet, I should feel touched.” But I just… couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure of it. It was more like this blank heaviness. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really happens much outside of that, no… or not in a way I can clearly point to. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus that it gets frightening—like I’m seeing everything through glass, and I’m not quite inside myself. The buildings and the students around me feel just a little unreal. I know that probably sounds odd, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, nothing I would want to label that way, I don’t think. I’ve had stressful things happen, like anyone, but I don’t know if they count as traumatic.\n\nIs it okay if I say I’m not sure?\n\nDoctor: Are there things from your past that you try to avoid thinking or talking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, there are things I don’t love dwelling on, but I wouldn’t say there’s something specific from my past that I’m actively avoiding talking about.\n\nI hope that’s an okay answer—I’m trying to be honest, I just don’t know that there’s anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're doing everyday activities at home?\n\nPatient: At home? Not really… or at least not in the same way. I might feel a little spaced out sometimes, but the thing that really scares me is when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem slightly unreal.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange. I’m sorry—I’m trying to explain it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings is triggered by something specific about walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I think it might be something about being out there in the middle of campus, yes. I don’t know exactly what triggers it, but when I’m walking between buildings and there are students around, that’s when it can suddenly feel like I’m behind glass or not quite in my body.\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it that way, and I don’t want to overstate it, but it really does scare me when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same way if you were walking alone in a quiet area?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, but I think it would probably be less intense if I were alone somewhere quiet. The part that really seems to scare me is crossing campus with the buildings and people around me — that’s when it starts feeling like I’m watching everything through glass, or like I’m not fully in my body.\n\nI know that sounds odd… I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings is related to feeling anxious or overwhelmed in that specific situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is related to feeling overwhelmed, at least in that situation. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but when I’m walking across campus, that’s what scares me most — it suddenly feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem a little unreal.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s the “right” way to describe it, but it does seem to happen when I’m out there and feeling kind of flooded by everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about walking across campus that you find particularly stressful or worrying?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but it’s hard to pin down exactly. It’s not one single thing like a particular building or spot, I don’t think. It’s more that when I’m walking across campus with everything around me — the buildings, the students passing by — that’s when I get really scared because it starts to feel like I’m watching it all through glass.\n\nAnd I feel a little embarrassed saying it, but it’s like I’m not fully in my own body, and everything around me seems just slightly unreal. I’m not sure if that answers the question properly, but that’s the part that worries me most.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about campus that triggers these feelings of disconnection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s one specific spot on campus, exactly. It’s more the whole feeling of being there — walking past the buildings, with students around — and then I start feeling like I’m seeing it all through glass.\n\nIt scares me because I don’t feel completely in my body, if that makes sense, and the buildings and people seem just a little unreal. I know that sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: It’s not all the time, exactly… mostly when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it hits me the most, like I’m seeing everything through glass or I’m not quite in my body. The buildings and students feel kind of unreal for a bit. I know that probably sounds odd, but it’s been happening enough that it’s scaring me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations when you feel like you're watching everything through glass?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure if it’s physical exactly. It’s more like… when I’m walking across campus, I suddenly feel like there’s this layer between me and everything else, like I’m watching through glass. I don’t feel fully in my own body, and the buildings and people around me seem kind of unreal for a moment. That’s the part that scares me most, honestly… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're walking across campus and everything feels unreal?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think anxious is fair. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s the part that scares me most—when I’m walking across campus and it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body. The buildings and students around me just feel slightly unreal, and then I get worried that something is really wrong with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body or things seem unreal when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least, not that I’ve noticed in the same way. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students seem a little unreal. I’m sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part that scares me most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience emotions as strongly as you used to?\n\nPatient: I… I think maybe not as strongly, no. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know people are trying to be kind. Like, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was really sweet. I could recognize it, if that makes sense. But I couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure I normally would. It was more like this blank heaviness. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel numb or disconnected from your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not all the time, but enough that I notice it. Like I said, my husband cut flowers from the garden and made me tea, and I knew that was kind—I could tell myself, “This is sweet, I should feel touched.” But I just… couldn’t really feel the warmth or pleasure of it. It was more like this blank heaviness. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're not walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really happens much outside of that, no… or not in a way I can clearly point to. It’s mostly when I’m walking across campus that it gets frightening—like I’m seeing everything through glass, and I’m not quite inside myself. The buildings and the students around me feel just a little unreal. I know that probably sounds odd, but that’s the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, nothing I would want to label that way, I don’t think. I’ve had stressful things happen, like anyone, but I don’t know if they count as traumatic.\n\nIs it okay if I say I’m not sure?\n\nDoctor: Are there things from your past that you try to avoid thinking or talking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, there are things I don’t love dwelling on, but I wouldn’t say there’s something specific from my past that I’m actively avoiding talking about.\n\nI hope that’s an okay answer—I’m trying to be honest, I just don’t know that there’s anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're doing everyday activities at home?\n\nPatient: At home? Not really… or at least not in the same way. I might feel a little spaced out sometimes, but the thing that really scares me is when I’m walking across campus. That’s when it feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem slightly unreal.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange. I’m sorry—I’m trying to explain it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings is triggered by something specific about walking across campus?\n\nPatient: I think it might be something about being out there in the middle of campus, yes. I don’t know exactly what triggers it, but when I’m walking between buildings and there are students around, that’s when it can suddenly feel like I’m behind glass or not quite in my body.\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it that way, and I don’t want to overstate it, but it really does scare me when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same way if you were walking alone in a quiet area?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, but I think it would probably be less intense if I were alone somewhere quiet. The part that really seems to scare me is crossing campus with the buildings and people around me — that’s when it starts feeling like I’m watching everything through glass, or like I’m not fully in my body.\n\nI know that sounds odd… I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings is related to feeling anxious or overwhelmed in that specific situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is related to feeling overwhelmed, at least in that situation. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but when I’m walking across campus, that’s what scares me most — it suddenly feels like I’m watching everything through glass, like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem a little unreal.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s the “right” way to describe it, but it does seem to happen when I’m out there and feeling kind of flooded by everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about walking across campus that you find particularly stressful or worrying?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but it’s hard to pin down exactly. It’s not one single thing like a particular building or spot, I don’t think. It’s more that when I’m walking across campus with everything around me — the buildings, the students passing by — that’s when I get really scared because it starts to feel like I’m watching it all through glass.\n\nAnd I feel a little embarrassed saying it, but it’s like I’m not fully in my own body, and everything around me seems just slightly unreal. I’m not sure if that answers the question properly, but that’s the part that worries me most.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about campus that triggers these feelings of disconnection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s one specific spot on campus, exactly. It’s more the whole feeling of being there — walking past the buildings, with students around — and then I start feeling like I’m seeing it all through glass.\n\nIt scares me because I don’t feel completely in my body, if that makes sense, and the buildings and people seem just a little unreal. I’m sorry, I know I’ve probably said that a few times, but that’s the part that feels most specific to campus.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything traumatic or very stressful related to the campus or university in the past?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was something at the university. I guess I’ve been trying not to make too much of it, because other people saw it too, and I know I should probably be handling it better.\n\nThere was this incident with a rig coming down, and now the sound of it keeps… barging into my head, I guess. Even when I’m just grading or making coffee, suddenly I can hear it again, and I can see the volunteer on the floor, and the students pushing forward, like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean by traumatic, but it was very stressful.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being disconnected from your body and surroundings when walking across campus is a way for you to deal with memories or feelings related to the incident with the rig coming down?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yes. I don’t know if I’m explaining it right, but when I’m walking across campus, what scares me most is that it suddenly feels like I’m watching everything through glass.\n\nIt’s like I’m not quite in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem a little unreal. I guess it could be my mind trying to deal with what happened, or keep it at a distance. I’m not totally sure, but that’s what it feels like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it probably is, or at least it’s connected. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when it happens most clearly — when I’m walking across campus, I suddenly feel like I’m watching everything through glass.

It’s scary because I don’t feel fully in my own body, and the buildings and students around me seem kind of unreal. So yes, I think the rig incident is probably the main reason, if that makes sense.
